# God.

---

->If you know what God is, you will be the more joyful. <note>original: "Αν γνῶς τί ἐστι Θεὸς, ἡδίων ἔσῃ." This Greek epigraph sets the tone for the book, suggesting that a philosophical understanding of the divine leads to inner peace and happiness.</note><-

## Preface.
---
It has been ten or twelve years since I
carried about with me a small treatise, <note>The German term "Schrift" refers to a written work, essay, or manuscript.</note>
which was to bear the title: Spinoza, Shaf-
tesbury, <note>original: "Shaftesburi"; Anthony Ashley-Cooper, 3rd Earl of Shaftesbury (1671–1713), was an English philosopher influential in ethics and aesthetics.</note> and Leibniz. It

One would greatly misunderstand their purpose if they were held merely as a <term>defense of honor <gloss>Ehrenrettung: a specific genre of Enlightenment writing aimed at rehabilitating the reputation of a misunderstood or maligned thinker.</gloss></term> for Spinoza; Spinoza does not need this defense of honor, and he should, in my

...[view, and according to my] purpose, now merely become the handle of a sacrificial vessel <note>original: "Opfergefäß." The author uses religious imagery to describe his work as a sacred tribute or a "libation" to his own intellectual past.</note>, from which I wished to offer a few drops to the altar of my youth. Why I took him <note>Referring to the philosopher Baruch Spinoza.</note> as my starting point lay partly in the sequence of my own thoughts, and partly in the occasions that my own era <note>A reference to the "Pantheism Controversy" involving Jacobi and Mendelssohn that was then consuming German intellectual life.</note> presented to me.

However, let no one interpret my writing as if I intended to step before or intervene in <note>The German "vor- oder zwischentreten" suggests an unwanted interruption or meddling in current affairs.</note> any current philosophy, to displace it, to challenge factions <note>original: "Partheien," meaning philosophical schools or parties of thought.</note>, or...

<summary>The author frames his study of Spinoza as a personal "offering" and a product of his own intellectual development rather than a polemical entry into contemporary philosophical disputes. He clarifies that he does not wish to displace existing systems or incite conflict between rival philosophical schools.</summary>

---

between factions <note>original: "Partheien." In the context of the late 18th century, this refers to the competing schools of thought, such as the Kantians, the followers of Spinoza, and the traditional rationalists.</note> to become an uninvited arbitrator <note>original: "unberuffener Schiedsrichter." Someone who intervenes in a legal or intellectual dispute without having the authority or being asked to do so.</note>. These are conversations between a few persons who express their opinions with the same right with which any other person presents his own doctrines <term>Lehrsätze: formal philosophical principles or dogmas presented as established truths</term>. Conversations <note>The author chose the dialogue format (popularized by writers like Lessing) to allow for a more exploratory, less confrontational exploration of ideas.</note> are not final decisions, and even less do they intend to stir up strife; for concerning God, I shall never quarrel.

I wished more ardently that what could only be hinted at here in conversation might find a [form] more appropriate to our philosophy—

# First Conversation.

---

### Philolaus.

Look, my friend, at this refreshing hour that follows the terrible thunderstorm. Sulfurous clouds <note>The "sulfurous clouds" serve as a metaphor for the stifling and frightening atmosphere of the controversial philosophical debates of the late 18th century.</note> towered above, robbing us of the sight of the sun and making every earthly thing breathe heavily; they have been shattered, and now everything breathes light and joy again. This is how I imagine the state of wisdom, back when Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a Dutch philosopher whose "Pantheism"—the idea that God and the universe are one—was often attacked as a form of atheism.</note> and those like him tried to rob the world of the sight of God with their heavy vapors: they, too, piled up toward the heavens—

---

<summary>This page opens the first dialogue of the book, using the metaphor of a clearing storm to describe the "dark" and "heavy" influence of Spinoza's philosophy on the world's understanding of God.</summary>

<keywords>Philolaus, Spinoza, God, nature, thunderstorm, enlightenment, philosophy, metaphors of light and dark</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 1, completing the sentence where Philolaus compares Spinoza's philosophy to storm clouds piling up toward the heavens. The first word "mel" is the conclusion of "Himmel" (heavens).</meta>

...wards the heavens and enshrouded the firmament; but a healthier philosophy has cast them down like the giants <note>A reference to the Gigantomachy in Greek mythology, where the giants attempted to storm Olympus and were struck down by the gods; here, Philolaus implies that Spinoza’s "monstrous" ideas were rightfully defeated by better logic.</note>, and the pondering spirit beholds the radiant sun once more.

**Theophron.** Have you read Spinoza, dear friend?

**Philolaus.** I have not read him; for who would want to read every obscure book by a madman? But I have heard from the mouths of many who *have* read him that he was an atheist and pantheist, a teacher of blind necessity <term>blind necessity: the philosophical concept that all events are determined by cold, mechanical laws without any divine purpose or "free will"</term>, an enemy of revelation, a mocker of religion, and thus a subverter of states and all civil society—in short, an enemy of the human race, and that he died as such. He therefore deserves the hatred and loathing of all friends of humanity and true philosophers.

**Theophron.** Yet the storm cloud with which you just com-
ver; <note>The catchword "ver;" is the beginning of the word "verglichen" (compared), which will be completed on the following page.</note>

<summary>Philolaus expresses the common 18th-century prejudice against Spinoza, labeling him a dangerous atheist and "enemy of the human race" despite admitting he has never actually read Spinoza’s work. Theophron begins to challenge this harsh characterization.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Philolaus, Theophron, atheism, pantheism, blind necessity, reputation, 18th-century philosophy, prejudice</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 2, completing the sentence where Theophron discusses the metaphor of the storm cloud. The word "verglichen" (compared) is completed here.</meta>

A 2

pared him: For it, too, belongs to the natural order and is salutary and useful. But, to speak without metaphors, have you, my friend, read nothing more specific or definite about Spinoza that we might hold onto in our conversation?

**Philolaus.** Much; for example, the article about him in Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a famous French philosopher. His *Historical and Critical Dictionary* contained a massive entry on Spinoza that, while critical, was for many years the primary source through which people learned about Spinoza’s ideas.</note>.

**Theophron.** In Bayle you have not exactly found the best authority this time. He, to whom all systems <term>system: in 18th-century philosophy, a "system" refers to a complete, logical framework that explains all of reality or knowledge</term> were matters of indifference because he basically had no system of his own, did not remain indifferent regarding Spinoza. He took an eager side against him, to which he was no doubt prompted by the circumstances of time and place. Perhaps he lived too close to the deceased: the teachings, indeed even the very name of Spinoza, was a slur <note>original: "Schimpfwort"</note> back then, as they largely remain even now: everything absurd and godless was called and cal-

<summary>Theophron challenges Philolaus's reliance on Pierre Bayle's critique of Spinoza, arguing that Bayle was biased by the social and religious pressures of his time, when Spinoza's name was synonymous with atheism.</summary>

<keywords>Spinoza, Pierre Bayle, philosophical systems, prejudice, historical context, slur, atheism, Enlightenment debate</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Theophron and Philolaus regarding the philosopher Spinoza and his critic Pierre Bayle. It picks up mid-word from the previous page's final sentence ("cal-led").</meta>

led, at least in part, as Spinozist. Now, it was hardly the business of such a subtle <term>dialectician: a thinker who uses logical debate and the investigation of opposing arguments to find the truth</term> as Bayle to fathom a system as a system, or to embrace it with the deepest sense of truth. He skimmed through all systems, shrewdly picking out their differences insofar as they served his doubts: one moment this opinion was important to him, the next another. But as for what is called inner philosophical conviction, his frivolous mind <note>original: "leichtes Gehirn" (literally "light brain"), suggesting someone who lacks intellectual depth or takes matters too lightly.</note> hardly had a concept of it—as his dictionary proves almost beyond contradiction.

**Philolaus.** Very true; and I have often wondered how such a keen mind could be so unsteady and disjointed in its opinions. One moment this absurdity is just as important to him as that one: a falsely cited date in Moréri <note>Louis Moréri (1643–1680). Bayle wrote his famous Dictionary specifically to correct the errors he found in Moréri's earlier biographical encyclopedia.</note> and the question of whether there is a God, how many such beings there might be, from where evil in the world originates, and the like—all occupy him with equal interest.

<summary>Theophron and Philolaus critique Pierre Bayle, arguing that his skeptical and inconsistent nature prevented him from truly understanding Spinoza’s philosophical system. They characterize Bayle as a brilliant but shallow thinker who treated historical trivia and profound theological questions with the same level of detached curiosity.</summary>

<keywords>Pierre Bayle, Spinozism, dialectics, philosophical conviction, skepticism, Louis Moréri, existence of God, problem of evil, historical criticism</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Theophron and Philolaus. The previous page ended with Philolaus noting Bayle's tendency to treat profound theological questions and trivial historical errors with the same level of interest. **Theophron.** Say rather, with equally little interest; but with all the more dexterity of the most ingenious play of thought. Precisely this constitutes Bayle’s <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706) was a French philosopher and skeptic. His "Historical and Critical Dictionary" was a massive, influential work of the early Enlightenment.</note> rare advantage. Name me another writer whose soul, in the lightest play, could embrace or touch upon so many and such various things with equal grace and equal attention? He was the philosophical-historical Voltaire of his day, whose fascination extended from the most sublime subject to the smallest detail of a historical circumstance, an anecdote, a book title, or even an off-color joke. Spinoza’s system <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) was a Dutch philosopher who argued that God and Nature are one and the same, a philosophy often called "Spinozism" or pantheism.</note> was not at all suited for a mind of this kind. This reclusive, heavy <note>original: "schwere," which in this context implies a thinker who is profound, serious, and perhaps somewhat ponderous or difficult to read.</note> thinker had a very contemptuous concept of everything that was merely opinion, and pursued pure, dry truth with mathematical precision <note>Spinoza famously wrote his "Ethics" in a "geometric order," using axioms, definitions, and proofs similar to a math textbook.</note> wherever he believed he might find it. Everything else he forgot; and of Bayle’s erudition, of his wit and

<summary>Theophron compares Pierre Bayle to a "Voltaire of his day," noting that Bayle’s playful, wandering intellect was fundamentally incompatible with Spinoza’s rigorous, mathematical pursuit of absolute truth.</summary>

<keywords>Theophron, Pierre Bayle, Baruch Spinoza, intellectual history, Enlightenment, philosophical systems, erudition, mathematical precision</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Theophron and Philolaus regarding the contrast between Pierre Bayle and Baruch Spinoza. The previous page ended mid-sentence, noting that of Bayle's wit and acuity...</meta>

acuity of mind he perhaps did not have one-thousandth as much. Two minds of such a different nature will hardly do justice to one another; and yet I am convinced that Spinoza would have been more likely to do justice to the author of the *Dictionary* <note>The "Dictionary" refers to Pierre Bayle’s massive and influential *Dictionnaire Historique et Critique* (1697).</note> than the lively, bustling Bayle was inclined to do for Spinoza. During his lifetime, Bayle was already accused of not having correctly understood Spinoza’s system, and he defended himself against this charge in a letter of his own. <insert>a)</insert>

**Philolaus.** That is unfortunate for Spinoza then; for it is Bayle, after all, who has defined the concept that the vast majority <note>original: "grössesten Haufen," literally "the largest heap," a common Enlightenment-era term for the general public or the "unlearned masses."</note> holds of him. How few read Spinoza’s obscure writings, while all the world reads the immensely useful, varied, and pleasant Bayle.

**Theophron.** Precisely so, my friend. For the light army of readers,

a) *Works of Bayle* <note>original: "Oeuvr. de Bayle" (Oeuvres de Bayle).</note> Vol. IV, pp. 169, 170.

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Theophron and Philolaus regarding the historical reception of Spinoza's philosophy. The previous page established that while Pierre Bayle provided the "popular" definition of Spinoza for casual readers, this page explores how he was treated by more serious academic and religious circles.</meta>

Bayle fixed the concept of Spinoza; for the heavy phalanx <note>original: "schweren Phalanx." A military metaphor referring to systematic, academic thinkers and scholars, contrasted with the "light army" of casual readers mentioned on the previous page.</note>, it was mostly contentious philosophers and theologians who did so, and there he fared even worse. It happened to him according to the Gospel <note>original: "nach dem Evangelio." A reference to Matthew 10:36: "A man's enemies will be the members of his own household."</note>: those of his own household first became his worst enemies—the Cartesians <note>Followers of the French philosopher René Descartes (1596–1650). Spinoza began as a Cartesian before developing his own system.</note>. They wanted and were forced to separate their philosophy—from which he had started and whose vocabulary he used—from his own, so that they too would not fall under the suspicion of Spinozism; naturally, this philosophical caution spread from Descartes' school to every subsequent one. Then the theologians of almost all denominations <note>original: "Confessionen," referring to the various branches of Christianity such as Lutheran, Reformed, and Catholic.</note> attacked him even more bitterly; for he had not only expressed very free opinions about Judaism and the books of the Old Testament, but what must have seemed much worse to them, he had primarily taken up his pen against them. He attributed a large part of the

decline of Christianity to the ineffectiveness of its most beautiful dogmas; and although he did this without any bitterness, you can easily imagine the reception of his book. <note>This refers to Spinoza’s "Tractatus Theologico-Politicus" (1670), a foundational text of modern biblical criticism and political theory.</note>

**Philolaus.** I can see it clearly. A peacemaker without credentials <note>original: "Vollmacht." Refers to an official mandate or authority to act.</note> needs only to step between heated factions and he will have both against him.

**Theophron.** Spinoza had no credentials other than those he believed he had received from the hands of fairness <note>original: "Billigkeit." A term implying equity, justice, and reasonableness.</note> and truth; but he certainly did not use them in a worldly-wise <note>original: "weltkluge." Referring to being politically savvy or cautious in public life.</note> manner. He made his religious-political views known in a work whose theology was bound to enrage both Jews and Christians; indeed, his political principles were so rigorous and uncompromising <note>original: "schnurgerade," literally "straight as a string."</note> that they certainly could not be accepted in those times. To the state, he granted full authority over the arrangement

ment of external worship: to reason, he reserved the unrestricted freedom to use its powers; to most people, both seemed so extreme that it was as if he wanted to mix fire and water. His theory, therefore, necessarily had to fail: for in many ways it still seems too harsh to us today, and in a sense too Hobbesian <note>Referring to Thomas Hobbes (1588–1679), whose political philosophy emphasized absolute sovereign power to prevent social chaos.</note>, even though we have made great strides in tolerance and statesmanship. Locke, Bayle, Shaftesbury, and others proceeded more quietly.

**Philolaus.** And yet they too had to endure enough before their most reasonable <note>original: "billigsten." In this context, it refers to ideas that are fair, equitable, or logically sound rather than "cheap."</note> propositions were generally recognized. In such dangerous matters, a debating dialectician <term>dialectician: a philosopher who uses the art of logical discussion and investigation of truths through debate</term> like Bayle, or a poet who cloaks his ideas like Voltaire, certainly has many advantages over the serious philosopher who sets down his propositions directly. The former always remain safer, because they can say: "I was only debating, only cloaking my meaning"; and yet they work in this [way]...

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Philolaus and Theophron regarding the influence of philosophers. It picks up from page 9, completing the thought that thinkers who "cloak" their ideas in literature or debate often reach a wider audience than those who write strict philosophical treatises.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

...pleasant, ever-changing cloak all the more universally. Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a French philosopher known for his *Historical and Critical Dictionary*, which promoted religious skepticism and tolerance.</note> certainly had more of an effect on his age than Spinoza and Leibniz; Voltaire more than Rousseau and a hundred even more rigorous philosophers.

**Theophron.** It depends on how you look at it, Philolaus; there is both an external and an internal effect. The former spreads far and wide; the latter takes root all the more firmly. I wish that a philosophically critical man—no mere youth—would in our time publish Spinoza’s *Theological-Political Treatise* <note>original: "theologisch: politischen Versuch." This refers to Spinoza’s *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* (1670), a foundational text for modern biblical criticism and political liberalism.</note> with annotations. It would be a useful endeavor to see what time has confirmed or refuted in it. In the critique of the writings of the Old Testament, many have since presented things as "new discoveries"—and far more imperfectly—which were already more thoroughly established in Spinoza. On the matter of tolerance, the nature of our states has been able to take almost no other path than the one Spinoza then [prescribed]...

<meta>catchword: allge-</meta>

<summary>Theophron argues that while literary philosophers like Voltaire reach more people, rigorous thinkers like Spinoza have a deeper, more lasting impact, particularly in the fields of biblical criticism and political tolerance.</summary>

<keywords>Pierre Bayle, Baruch Spinoza, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, Voltaire, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Theological-Political Treatise, Old Testament, Religious Tolerance, Biblical Criticism</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues a dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron regarding Baruch Spinoza's controversial "Theological-Political Treatise." <page-num>11</page-num>

...marked out for general hatred. To be sure, in this work, as in all his other writings, everything is stated harshly and much is exaggerated. Regarding the poetry of the prophets <note>Spinoza’s *Tractatus Theologico-Politicus* argued that prophecy was a product of the imagination rather than purely rational intellect, a view that deeply offended traditional theologians of the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>, for example, he had only a metaphysical sense, and in the entire composition of his work, he is a solitary thinker to whom the grace of social interaction and an ingratiating delivery is entirely unknown.

**Philolaus.** It surprises me, Theophron, that you place such value on it: for how could a person without sound principles—an atheist, a pantheist, and so on—write on any subject in a way that would be accepted by rational people? He is even said to have wanted to demonstrate pantheism and atheism; what could be more nonsensical?

**Theophron.** So, both atheism and pantheism? <note>In the 18th century, "Pantheist" was often used as a slur synonymous with "Atheist," though they represent different views: one claiming God is everything, the other claiming there is no God.</note> But how are both possible in one and the same system? The pantheist

<summary>Philolaus dismisses Spinoza’s work based on his reputation as an atheist and pantheist, while Theophron begins to challenge the logical contradiction of labeling someone as both simultaneously.</summary>

<keywords>Baruch Spinoza, Philolaus, Theophron, atheism, pantheism, biblical prophecy, metaphysics, Enlightenment dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron. The previous page ended mid-sentence with the first half of the word "pantheist." This page picks up with the conclusion of that thought, distinguishing between the denial of God and the misunderstanding of God's nature.</meta>

...theist still always has a God, even if he is mistaken about God's nature; the atheist, on the other hand, who simply denies God, can be neither a pantheist nor a polytheist, unless one is just playing with words. Furthermore, my friend, how can one prove atheism—that is, a negation?

**Philolaus.** Why not? If one discovered, or believed they had discovered, an internal contradiction in the nature of God.

**Theophron.** An internal contradiction in a simple, supreme concept, the highest of which humanity is capable? I confess that I understand nothing of the sort.

**Philolaus.** That is why he was also a fool <note>original: *Unsinniger*—literally "one without sense" or "nonsensical person."</note> who wanted to demonstrate what could not be demonstrated: for our new philosophy <note>This likely refers to the "Critical Philosophy" of Immanuel Kant, which was gaining dominance at the time and argued that God's existence could not be proven by theoretical reason alone.</note> states loudly: "Neither that a God exists, nor that He does not exist, can be demonstrated. The former one must believe."

<vocab>God, Atheist, Pantheist, Polytheist, Atheism, Negation, Philolaus, Theophron, Contradiction, Philosophy, Demonstrate, Believe</vocab>

**Theophron.** At the very least, I should think that one must believe one of the two; that it is therefore left to our discretion to be atheists, deists, or theists, depending on our faith. In philosophy, we have moved past the era of the "honorary titles" <note>Theophron uses the term "honorary titles" sarcastically to refer to the vicious insults and labels once common in academic and theological debate.</note> with which Spinoza was still labeled by Korthold <note>Christian Kortholt (1633–1694), a Lutheran theologian who wrote "The Three Great Impostors," identifying Spinoza as one of them.</note>, Brucker <note>Johann Jakob Brucker (1696–1770), a German historian of philosophy whose work was widely read during the Enlightenment.</note>, and others. The former thought himself witty when he turned the name *Benedictus* into *Maledictus* <note>original: *Benedictus* (Blessed) into *Maledictus* (Accursed). This is a play on Spinoza's first name.</note> and the word *Spinoza* into a thorny bramble <note>original: *stachlichten Dornbusch*. This is a pun on the Latin word *spina*, which means "thorn."</note>. For others, the names "impudent, godless, nonsensical, shameless, blasphemous, pestilential, abominable" are the common adjectives with which they summon him from the realm of spirits. One "Elect" <note>A reference to those who consider themselves the "chosen" or "Elect" of God, often within a strict Calvinist or dogmatic context.</note> even claimed to see the mark of eternal rejection <note>original: *Verwerfung*—reprobation or damnation in a theological sense.</note> upon his face—

A remarkable man, this Spinoza. Wherever he may have gotten his thoughts and whatever they may be, there is something constant in his whole life. He devotes himself to Jewish theology and then leaves it in order to learn natural science <note>original: *Naturlehre*; referring to the study of the physical world and laws of nature.</note> thoroughly: the works of Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher whose rationalist methods deeply influenced Spinoza.</note> fall into his hands, and since he has read them with singular eagerness and later confesses that whatever philosophical knowledge he possesses, he has drawn from them: thus he quietly turns away from Judaism, because he believes himself convinced that he can no longer follow its dogmas. He is offered an annual pension of a thousand guilders <note>original: *Gulden*; a substantial sum offered by the community leaders to maintain a public appearance of orthodoxy.</note> just so that he might continue to visit the synagogue; he rejects it and withdraws without a sound into the stillness. He is placed under the ban <note>original: *Bann*; the *cherem* or formal excommunication issued against Spinoza by the Amsterdam Sephardic community in 1656.</note>: he responds to it and, in the stillness, learns a trade <note>original: *Handthierung*; Spinoza famously supported himself by grinding and polishing optical lenses.</note> to sustain himself. Spinoza’s landlord assures us that this portrait looked very much like the man himself. It was a strange fancy to draw himself as Masaniello <note>Tommaso Aniello, or Masaniello, was a fisherman who led a brief, violent revolt in Naples in 1647. Now he grinds lenses; his friends sell them, and he lives frugally; often, for two or three days, he sees no one. Many offer him their purse and their help, but he politely declines everything; he lives on simple food and settles his accounts every quarter, simply so that he does not spend more than he has to spend. He is, as he tells his housemates, like a snake that makes a circle with its tail in its mouth <note>The Ouroboros, an ancient symbol of a serpent eating its own tail. I have seen this symbol beneath his portrait and foolishly interpreted it as a reference to his pantheism <note>Pantheism is the philosophical view that the universe and God are identical. He wishes to collect no more than what is necessary to live with prosperity...

His most sincere friend offers him a gift of two thousand **guilders** <term>guilder: the primary currency of the Netherlands at the time; 2,000 guilders was a very significant sum</term> so that he might live with somewhat more comfort; he politely declines it. That same friend wishes to name Spinoza as his heir; he does not accept this benevolence and reduces the **annuity** <term>annuity: a fixed sum of money paid to someone each year, often used as a pension</term> that the friend insists on giving him during his final years by nearly half. 

Thus he lives, and dies in his forty-fifth year just as gently and peacefully as he had lived. A few hours beforehand, he had held a long conversation with the people of the house about the **sermon** <note>Spinoza frequently discussed theology with his landlord's family, despite his own controversial views.</note> they had heard, and before they could leave for church in the afternoon, he turned pale <note>original: *erblaßt*, a delicate way of saying he passed away</note> in the presence of his physician.

can be reduced to three things: **wealth**, **honor**, and **sensual pleasure**. By all three, however, the **disposition** <note>original: *Gemüth*; this refers to the mind's internal state or mood.</note> is so distracted that it cannot think of any other good. For as far as **sensual pleasure** is concerned, it deceives the mind for a time, as if it were resting in a [true] good, and thus prevents it from thinking of any other; but soon after its enjoyment, the deepest **sadness** follows, which, if it does not imprison the **spirit**, at least disturbs and dulls it. Even when we pursue honor and wealth, the **soul** is distracted, especially when we desire them for their own sake, because they then seem to us to be a highest good. **Honor**, however, distracts the mind even more than wealth, because it is continually valued as a true good and as the ultimate end according to which everything must be directed. Furthermore, in the case of honor and wealth, **repentance** <note>original: *Reue*; Spinoza is contrasting the "hangover" or immediate regret of physical pleasure with the more persistent, addictive nature of social and financial ambition.</note> does not occur as it does with sensual pleasure:

<meta>This page continues the translation of Baruch Spinoza’s "Treatise on the Emendation of the Intellect." On the previous page, Spinoza reflected on the difficulty of abandoning worldly pursuits—wealth and honor—which are "certain goods" in a social sense, for a philosophical "good" that is still uncertain to him. He was in a state of indecision about whether to change his life completely.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

...had, I first found that if I exchanged my old **way of life** for the new, I would still only be giving up a **good** that is uncertain by its nature <note>Spinoza argues that wealth and social status are "uncertain" because they depend on luck and the whims of others.</note> for another uncertain thing—one which, however, by its nature could not be uncertain because I was seeking a stable **good**; rather, it remained doubtful only in whether I would reach it. Through continued **reflection**, however, I came so far as to realize that if I could only bring my deliberation to a **resolution**, I would be exchanging certain evils for a certain **good**. I saw, in fact, that I was in the greatest danger and felt the necessity to seek even an uncertain **remedy** with all my strength: like a sick person who, if he uses no medicine, sees certain death before him and must therefore seek even an uncertain **remedy** with all his strength, since his entire **hope** rests upon it. But all those things that the great multitude strives for, provi— <note>original: *gewäh:*; likely the beginning of *gewähren* (to provide or grant).</note>

**Theophron.** First and almost exclusively his *Ethics*. The rest consists of fragments, and the *Theological-Political Treatise* was merely an earlier occasional publication <note>original: *Zeitschrift*. Theophron implies that this famous work was a product of the specific political and religious controversies of Spinoza's time, rather than a permanent philosophical system.</note>. But by all means, take a few rules with you on your journey.

1. Before you read Spinoza, you must necessarily read Descartes, even if only as a dictionary. In him, you will see the origin of the words and thoughts, and thus also the source of Spinoza’s strange, harsh expressions. To this end, take Descartes’ main writings or those of one of his students, *a)* among whom Clauberg <note>Johannes Clauberg (1622–1665) was a leading German Cartesian philosopher who sought to reconcile Descartes' ideas with traditional logic.</note> in particular presents the principles of <term>Cartesianism</term> <note>The philosophical system of René Descartes, characterized by a focus on reason, the separation of mind and body, and a mathematical approach to nature.</note> very clearly and methodically; you will find these here in a single volume—

3. Never stop with him alone; rather, for every one of his paradoxical sentences, call upon more recent philosophy for assistance. Ask yourself how later thinkers have cleared away such a claim, or how they have expressed a similar assertion more easily, better, more inoffensively, or more successfully. Immediately, it will become clear to you why your author was not yet able to express himself so fortunately; thus, you will perceive the origin of his error and the progress of truth itself. With this intention, add to your study his few letters regarding his morality: *a)* they are very enlightening in many respects, and in the margins of my own copy, you will find references to the *Ethics* <term>Ethics</term> <note>Spinoza's masterpiece, the *Ethica*, which was published posthumously.</note>—and in the *Ethics* back to the letters—written by an old hand. He does not occupy places by being 
contained within any regions or 
enclosed by any limits, but free, 
spread out <note>original: *diditus*; the author uses a rarer Latin form for "distributed" or "spread."</note> into every space, he wanders. 
His high power is his will, 
his work is his unchanging desire; 
he is great without quantity 
and good without quality. <note>These are classic scholastic descriptions of God: "Great without quantity" means God is infinite but not composed of physical parts; "Good without quality" means God is goodness itself, rather than having goodness as an accidental trait.</note>
What he speaks, he completes in a single moment;

When he spoke, behold, with his voice

He gazes upon all, perceives everything, 
and alone in one (for he alone is all), 
he himself, in his eternity, foresees 
what is, what has been, and what is yet to come.

<meta>This page begins the "Second Dialogue" between Philolaus and Theophron. In the previous section, they concluded a discussion on Vanini's poetic views of the Divine. Now, Philolaus turns the conversation toward Baruch Spinoza, whose works he has been studying to determine if the philosopher truly deserves the label of "atheist" so often applied to him by his contemporaries.</meta>

## Second Dialogue.

### Philolaus.

I come with my Spinoza; yet almost more uncertain than I was before. That he is no atheist appears on every page; the idea of God is for him the first and last—indeed, I might say, the only one of all ideas—as he binds to it the knowledge of the world and nature, the consciousness of himself and all things around him, his ethics, and his politics. Without the concept of God, his soul is capable of nothing—not even of thinking of itself—and it is nearly incomprehensible to him how men can make God, as it were, merely a consequence of other truths and even of sensory observations, since all truth, like all existence, follows only from the eternal truth, from the infinite, eternal existence of God. a) This concept is so [essential] to him—

---

a) See *Ethics*, page 49, scholium <note>original: *schol.*—Spinoza’s *Ethics* is written in a geometric style, featuring "scholia" or explanatory notes to his propositions.</note>, and letters 21, 39, 40, 49, etc. <note>original: *v. Ethic. p. 49. schol. et epist. 21. 39. 40. 49. etc.*</note>

<summary>Philolaus opens the second dialogue by arguing that Spinoza is not an atheist, as God serves as the foundational pillar for all of Spinoza's philosophical systems. He notes that for Spinoza, God is the primary truth from which all other knowledge and existence must necessarily flow.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, atheism, idea of God, Ethics, epistemology, Philolaus, existence, eternal truth</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues Philolaus’s opening argument from the Second Dialogue. The previous page ended with the assertion that the concept of God is essential to Spinoza; this page completes that thought, transitioning from the catchword "ten" (likely from a word like *wesentlichen*) into the description of how "present" this idea is in Spinoza's mind.</meta>

...so present, so immediate and intimate to him, that I would certainly consider him more an <term>enthusiast</term> <note>original: *Schwärmer* — in the 18th century, this term referred to someone with an intense, almost fanatical or mystical devotion to an idea.</note> for the existence of God than a doubter or denier of it. In the knowledge and love of God, he places all human perfection, virtue, and happiness; and that this is not some assumed mask, but rather his deepest feeling, is shown by his letters—indeed, I might say, by every part of his philosophical edifice, every line of his writings. He may have erred in his idea of God in a thousand ways; but how readers of his works could ever say that he denied the idea of God and demonstrated **atheism** is incomprehensible to me.

**Theophron.** I am glad, my friend, that you have found the same thing that I did: for I too almost distrusted my own judgment when I read this author and compared my own feelings with what others said about him.

<summary>Philolaus argues that Spinoza’s writings reveal a deep, sincere devotion to God, making the common accusation of atheism appear baseless. Theophron admits he felt the same surprise and relief upon reading the original texts for himself.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, existence of God, atheism, philosophical system, Philolaus, Theophron, virtue, happiness</keywords>

gain ground. If, however, the **Leibnizian school** dared to call **matter** an "appearance of substances"; why should **Spinoza** not be permitted his harsher expression? <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Theophron and Philolaus regarding the philosophy of Benedict de Spinoza. On the previous page, Philolaus argued that God, as an omnipresent and unchanging being, cannot be a "transient cause" (an external creator who acts and then moves on), but must be an "immanent cause" (a presence that remains within everything it creates).</meta>

**Philolaus.** Where is there a place outside the world? > How God once mused through eternity in solitude?
> Why now, and not before He had made a world? <note>These verses reflect the philosophical struggles of the 18th century, particularly seen in poets like Albrecht von Haller, who questioned how a timeless God relates to a world that has a beginning and an end.</note>

Or:

> — How the vast circles
> Of beginningless duration secretly travel their course?
> How the Eternal became time, and how the river of time
> Must one day lose itself in the sea of eternity? etc.

<summary>Philolaus argues that God cannot exist "outside" the world because space and time themselves are products of God’s infinite nature. Theophron notes that this perspective avoids the confusing theological puzzles regarding God’s existence before creation.</summary>

A beginningless duration is not the eternity of God, and within this eternity, there is no journey. The eternal can no more become time than time can become eternity, or the finite become the infinite.

[indefinitely] determined and the infinite in itself had correctly distinguished. <margin>a) See Epistle 29. <note>original: "v. Epist. 29." **Philolaus.** Is not space, is not time endless? What an uncountable multitude of divine forces and forms can thus reveal themselves within them! And since no two appearances can be the same according to place and time: what infinity springs from this ever-new, ever-rejuvenated source of divine beauty! Look out toward the heavens at those Milky Ways of suns and worlds. With his reflecting glass, the Columbus of our nation <note>A likely reference to the German-born astronomer William Herschel (1738–1822), who used powerful reflecting telescopes to discover Uranus and catalog thousands of nebulae.</note> is perhaps even now discovering new hosts of them in a small nebula <note>original: *Nebelwölkchen*, literally "little mist-cloud."</note> invisible to our eyes. <meta>This page continues Philolaus's critique of Leibniz's metaphysical theories, specifically addressing the "pre-established harmony" and how it relates to the systems of Descartes, Spinoza, and Geulincx.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

as it seems to me, why would that so forced hypothesis of **pre-established harmony** <note>original: *prästabilirte Harmonie*; Leibniz’s theory that the soul and body do not interact directly, but were synchronized by God at the beginning of time like two perfectly timed clocks.</note> between thought and matter—which, like two clocks, though acting in unison, run completely independent of one another—have been necessary? After all, his own concept of matter was animated by **immaterial forces**, upon which every higher kind of immaterial force could act; thus, the so-called **physical influence** <note>original: *physischer Einfluß*; the common-sense view that the mind and body have a real, causal impact on one another—a view Leibniz's system was designed to avoid.</note>—which nature shows us everywhere and against which no arbitrary hypothesis can prevail—was actually confirmed by his own system. 

God’s entire world becomes a realm of immaterial forces, none of which exists without a connection to others, because it is precisely from this connection and the mutual interaction of them all that the world's phenomena and changes arise. And with how much less sacrifice might **Leibniz** have taken this step! For his pre-established harmony was essentially already present as a flaw within **Cartesianism** <note>The philosophy of René Descartes, which famously struggled to explain how a non-physical mind could influence a physical body, leading later thinkers to propose elaborate workarounds.</note>; and **Spinoza** and **Geulincx** <note>Arnold Geulincx (1624–1669) was a follower of Descartes who argued for "Occasionalism," the idea that God acts as the intermediary for every single interaction between mind and body.</note>

<summary>Philolaus argues that Leibniz’s "pre-established harmony" was an unnecessary and forced complication, suggesting that Leibniz's own idea of "immaterial forces" actually supports the natural interaction between mind and matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Theophron and Philolaus regarding the relationship between philosophy and mathematics. The previous page ended with Philolaus noting that since Descartes, philosophy has sought to emulate the precision of mathematics.</meta>

page 79

**Theophron.** She <note>Philosophy</note> followed her <note>Mathematics</note> and learned from her guide everything that she could teach: clarity in concepts, precision in proofs, and order. But if the concepts are once grasped arbitrarily or abstracted incompletely, then all mathematically-pure representation of them, even in the best methodical order, is of no help. The proofs become pseudo-proofs; indeed, the rigorous form itself can become an obstacle to truth. We saw this with **Spinoza** <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), who famously wrote his *Ethics* "in the geometric manner" (*more geometrico*), using definitions, axioms, and propositions.</note>. With that one arbitrarily-assumed concept of matter, a multitude of other arbitrary explanations of <term>attributes</term> <note>original: "Attributen"; in Spinozism, these are the essential properties of God or Substance, such as thought and extension.</note>, <term>modifications</term> <note>original: "Modificationen"; also called 'modes,' these are the specific expressions or states of an attribute, such as an individual thought or a physical object.</note>, space, body, and so on, were brought about, which the mathematical method could not rectify. In literary criticism, there is a test: that what is nonsense in prose must also be nonsense in verse. In the same way, harsh expressions that cause offense in <term>unbound speech</term> <note>original: "ungebundner Rede"; a standard German term for prose.</note> cannot be rectified by geometric form alone—

**Theophron.** I can easily guess what that might be; and what if we were to find the most beautiful gold coin within that very knot?

The properties of creatures are all twofold: for just as they have power on one side, they have weakness on the other. If abundance is found in a thing, deficiency is also found within it. A beholder of God, an upright man, lowered his head to his chest and seemed submerged in the sea of contemplation. <note>The "sea of contemplation" is a common mystical metaphor where the soul "dives" into the depth of the Divine, losing awareness of the physical world.</note> When he emerged, one of his confidants addressed him and said: "What beautiful thing have you brought back for us from the garden in which you were?"

"I intended to pluck roses," he answered; "I wanted to fill my garment and my lap with them, as a gift for my friends; I already drew near to the bush full of beautiful, refreshing roses; but their powerful fragrance intoxicated and overwhelmed me; my garment slipped from my hand, and all the gathered roses fell away." <note>This allegory suggests that while a mystic intends to bring back "fruits" or "flowers" of wisdom to share, the actual experience of the Divine is so overwhelming that it destroys the capacity to carry anything back into the realm of language.</note>

Loud-singing nightingale, learn from the gnat what love is! The gnat flies into the beloved flame and its wings are scorched; dead and silent, it sinks down. <note>The nightingale represents the poet who talks and sings about love, while the gnat (often a moth in similar traditions) represents the true lover who is consumed by the object of its affection, reaching a union that ends in silence and death of the self.</note>

Those boasters, those chatterers about God, know nothing of Him; he who knows Him remains silent.

...greatly love. She is serious and beautiful: for she is a daughter of **Justice**, who can be nothing other than a wise goodness. Therefore, with her right hand, she measures the conduct and fortune of mortals and gazes impartially down toward her breast; but for the one who meets the measure, she holds out the branch of reward. Usually, she also has a wheel beneath her feet: a sign that she may topple the fortune of the arrogant in a swift instant, by the lightest touch, and bring him to ruin. In this statue, the artist left out this symbol and gave her instead only the quiet step and the gentle, firm posture that you noticed; our Nemesis, my friend <note>The abbreviation "m. He thus gives you your Nemesis, with her measuring arm and the branch in her hand, as a mathematical, physical, and metaphysical formula.

...scientific formulas. What common sense notices dimly, yet through intuition, in daily experiences, these formulas bring into the light. They trace these observations back to general laws—and where possible, to number and magnitude. Through this, their assertions gain the value of definite certainty and even a universal application, which one then gladly pursues in every individual object. Your **Lambert** <note>Johann Heinrich Lambert (1728–1777) was a Swiss polymath known for his work in mathematics, physics, and philosophy. In **mathematical physics**, many such laws and compensations of the highest wisdom have already been found. These exclude all arbitrary chance and give the thinking mind the sublime concept of **"inner perfection, goodness, and beauty in the existence and persistence of every single thing,"** to its indescribable joy. --- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues Theophron’s response to Philolaus. The catchword "Mög-" from the previous page concludes with "gen" to form the phrase "Indeed, one initially wanted to..." (Mögen hat man...).</meta>

Indeed, one initially wanted to conclude too much [from these observations]; however, that does not harm the beauty of their discovery. Error wears away, but the **truth** remains. The more **true physics** <note>Original: *wahre Physik*. In the 18th century, "physics" referred to natural philosophy—the study of the laws governing the physical world as a reflection of divine order.</note> advances, the further we move away from the realm of blind power and arbitrariness, and into the realm of the wisest necessity—a goodness and beauty that is firm within itself.

All senseless fear vanishes when a joyful, clear confidence perceives a **creation** everywhere, in whose smallest point the whole of God is present with His wisdom and goodness, and where He works according to the nature of that creature with His undivided and indivisible **divine power** <note>Original: *Gotteskraft*.</note>. Where, for example, is the empty terror that a **comet** <note>Original: *Comet*. Before their orbits were mathematically understood, comets were often feared as unpredictable omens of disaster.</note> might overtake our Earth? This fear has vanished since we have come to know the path of these celestial bodies more precisely; we have not only identified more than seventy of them, but have even calculated the specific instances in which such a collision might be feared according to **natural laws** <note>Original: *Naturgesetze*.</note>. The

The possibility of such a disaster <note>Refers to the potential collision of a comet with the Earth mentioned on the previous page.</note> becomes so incredibly small through calculation that, according to the inner nature of the forces by which the universe maintains itself, it almost vanishes into nothingness. What have people not imagined regarding the irregularities and their evil consequences, into which the celestial bodies would supposedly be plunged over time through their mutual attractions <note>Original: *gegenseitige Anziehungen*. This refers to gravitational pull between planets and stars.</note>! This empty terror has vanished solely through a clearer view of the matter itself, as it has been found that according to the immutable **laws of nature**, these irregularities compensate for one another. 

Benevolent, beautiful **necessity**, under whose widely extended scepter we live! She is a child of the highest wisdom, the twin sister of eternal power, the mother of all goodness, bliss, security, and order. If I knew of a more beautiful image of her from antiquity, I would use it; but **Nemesis** <note>In Greek mythology, the goddess of retribution who restores balance; here used to represent a corrective force.</note> should immediately yield her place to this higher **Adrastea** <note>Original: *Adrastea*. **Philolaus.** So that was the gold coin <note>A metaphor for a valuable insight or resolution.</note> you promised me within the knot that Spinoza tied for us with his "internal necessity of God’s nature"; but, Theophron, the knot is not yet untied. How harshly he speaks against all of God’s intentions in creation! How decisively he denies God intellect and will, and derives everything that exists solely and uniquely from His infinite power—a power he not only places above intellect and intentions, but also completely separates from them. You know, my friend, that these propositions have drawn the harshest opponents to our philosopher; even **Leibniz**, who held Spinoza in high regard, declared himself most firmly against them in his *Theodicy* <note>Original: *Theodicee*. Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz's 1710 work which argues that we live in the "best of all possible worlds" and defends God's justice against the problem of evil.</note>. If you can reconcile these so offensive propositions with sound reason <note>Original: *gesunde Vernunft*. Refers to "common sense" or rational judgment.</note> or with Spinoza's otherwise so excellent system, then I would wish to be **Nemesis** <note>The goddess of retribution and balance; Philolaus suggests he would trade his skepticism for a peaceful tribute if Theophron succeeds.</note> myself, offering you the branch.

<summary>Philolaus challenges Theophron to reconcile Spinoza’s concept of divine necessity with the traditional view of God as a being with intellect and will, noting that even Leibniz found Spinoza's denial of divine intention offensive.</summary>

<keywords>Philolaus, Spinoza, Leibniz, Theodicy, divine will, necessity, reason, Nemesis, creation</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 94, where Philolaus challenged Theophron to reconcile Spinoza’s denial of divine intellect and will with rational thought. Theophron now begins his defense, suggesting that Spinoza's perceived errors are actually baggage from Cartesian philosophy rather than flaws in his own core logic.</meta>

( 95 )

**Theophron.** I only wish to receive it from the hand of truth: for I can clearly prove, partly, that Spinoza did not fully understand himself in these propositions because they are consequences of the poor Cartesian <note>Refers to the philosophy of René Descartes (1596–1650). Theophron argues that Spinoza was still trapped by Descartes' definitions of matter and spirit.</note> explanations which he adopted into his system—and was obliged to adopt according to the spirit of his time; and partly that he has been understood far more incorrectly than he expressed himself obscurely. However, if we clear away those errors of **Descartes** and explain Spinoza’s propositions solely according to the fundamental idea upon which he built his own system, then they clear up; the mists drift away and Spinoza gains, it seems to me, even a step ahead of **Leibniz**, who followed him cautiously, but in this particular matter perhaps too cautiously.

**Philolaus.** I am very curious.

**Theophron.** First of all, I deny entirely that Spinoza imagined God to be a thoughtless being; it can hardly be an error <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "ge:" indicates the next word on the following page will likely be "gefordert" (demanded) or "gewesen" (been).</note>

<summary>Theophron begins his defense of Spinoza by arguing that the philosopher's most controversial claims were actually unintended leftovers from Cartesianism. He suggests that Spinoza’s true system, when properly understood, is more advanced than that of Leibniz.</summary>

**Philolaus.** So that was the piece of gold you promised me in the knot that **Spinoza** tied for us with his internal <term>necessity of the nature of God<gloss>The philosophical idea that God's actions are not the result of "free will" in the human sense, but flow inevitably from His own essence.</gloss></term>; but, **Theophron**, the knot is not yet untied. How decisively he denies God intellect and will, and derives everything that exists solely and exclusively from His infinite power, which he not only places above intellect and purpose, but also separates completely from them. You know, my friend, that these propositions have drawn the harshest opponents to our philosopher; even **Leibniz**, who held **Spinoza** in high regard, declared himself most decisively against them in his *Theodicy* <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716) wrote the *Essays on Theodicy* in 1710 to defend God's justice and wisdom against the charge that a perfect God could not create a world with evil.</note>. **Theophron.** Well then, according to Spinoza, God is essentially an infinite, primordial power of thought, the primal source of all thoughts; and according to this system, we have no reason to doubt the infinite efficacy <note>original: "Wirkungskraft," referring to the power to produce effects or take action.</note> within him.

**Philolaus.** No: for according to Spinoza, understanding and will are even one and the same. <note>In his *Ethics* (Part II, Proposition 49, Corollary), Spinoza argues that "the will and the intellect are one and the same thing," meaning God does not "choose" to act via a separate faculty of will, but rather acts through the necessity of his nature.</note> That is, in our gentler language: an understanding that perceives the best must also will the best, and if it has the power to do so, must bring it to effect. But there is no doubting the infinite power of his God, since he subjects everything to this very power and derives everything from it.

**Theophron.** What was he lacking, then, that he did not unite this infinite power of thought and efficacy, and in this union express more clearly what he necessarily had to find within it—namely: that the highest power is necessarily also the wisest power,

power—that is, an infinite goodness ordered according to internal, eternal laws? --- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron. Philolaus is reacting to a poem describing Leibniz’s theory of "possible worlds," arguing instead for a conception of God where existence and divine will are inseparable.</meta>

as it seems to me, without philosophical, God-worthy, pure truth. No sketches lay spread open before God; he did not sit like a brooding artist, racking his brains, drafting, comparing, rejecting, and choosing. No realm of the possible <note>This "realm of the possible" refers to the Leibnizian *regio possibilium*, a conceptual space where all non-contradictory ideas exist before God chooses to actualize them.</note> exists without and outside of God: for if He did not want to create it, or could not create it, then it was not possible. No world, let alone a thousand worlds based on enticing designs, which would have required only a nod to come into existence and yet which God did not choose, could ever have become a thought of God. He did not play with worlds as children play with soap bubbles, until one pleased him and he preferred it. If a thousand others besides this one were possible, then a greater God could have created them, and the weaker, laboriously-deliberating one was no God.

**Theophron.** Read on.

**Philolaus.**

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron regarding the intellectual relationship between Leibniz and Spinoza. The previous page ended with Philolaus criticizing the "word-clutter" that had been passed down from Leibniz’s metaphors and which...</meta>

is now sanctified by many as rigorous philosophy.

**Theophron.** By many, but certainly not by all. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron, specifically following the footnote regarding the philosophical works of Leibniz published by Raspe. It explores the distinction between Leibniz’s "moral necessity" and Spinoza’s concept of necessity.</meta>

<margin>Leibniz’s writings, of which, incidentally, every line is instructive.</margin>

**Philolaus.** And this particular system was —

**Theophron.** The system of **moral necessity** in God, according to which He chose the best out of **suitability** <term>suitability</term> <note>Original: "Convenienz." In Leibniz's philosophy, this is the principle of "fitness" or "becomingness," where God chooses what is most appropriate for the best possible world.</note>.

**Philolaus.** And how is moral necessity distinguished from our necessity—which I will call the essential, internal, divine necessity? God must fully perceive and effect the best, not through some weak **arbitrariness** <term>arbitrariness</term> <note>Original: "Willkühr." This refers to the idea of a choice made by a random whim or indifferent will, which Philolaus argues does not apply to God.</note>, but according to His nature, without a slow comparison with the worse—which, without Him, is a nothingness. Even in **Spinoza’s** system, there is no mention at all of a **physical necessity**, insofar as this implies a blind, external compulsion; Spinoza fights against such a notion with all his might.

<summary>The speakers distinguish between Leibniz’s "moral necessity," where God chooses the best based on fitness, and a more internal, divine necessity where God's nature and actions are one. They clarify that Spinoza’s philosophy rejects the idea of God being forced by external, blind "physical necessity."</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Spinoza, moral necessity, divine necessity, suitability, arbitrariness, physical necessity, divine nature</keywords>

...laws. However, God knows no moral laws from without—

**Theophron.** Leibniz was not thinking of those either when he chose the term **moral necessity**; he merely contrasted it with physical necessity—that is, with blind power or external compulsion—and, regarding the former, he took offense at the harsh expressions used by **Spinoza**. Therefore, those who attribute blind **fatalism** to Leibniz do him an injustice, it seems to me: he explained himself concisely on this matter against **Clarke** <note>Samuel Clarke (1675–1729), an English philosopher and disciple of Isaac Newton, famous for his debate with Leibniz over the nature of space, time, and God’s will.</note>, and he even mitigated his concept of moral necessity in God as much as he could through **anthropopathies** <term>anthropopathies</term> <note>Original: "Anthropopathieen." chance, just as little as the intellect of God Himself is wise by chance or good by chance. <meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Philolaus criticized the practice of assigning arbitrary divine "purposes" to astronomical phenomena. I 2

From these deceptions, for which the name of God is misused, the humble natural scientist escapes; he who does not proclaim particular expressions of will from the chamber of the divine council, but instead investigates the nature of things themselves and observes the laws essentially implanted within them. He seeks and finds—while he seems to forget God's intentions—the whole God in every object and point of creation; that is, in every thing he finds an essential truth, harmony, and beauty, without which it would not and could not be. Upon this, its existence is founded with an internal necessity—a necessity that is conditional, yet in its own way just as essential as that upon which the existence of God unconditionally rests. The very dependence of things on God makes their essences necessary reflections <note>Original: "Ebenbilder," literally "images" or "likenesses."</note> of His goodness and beauty, as these could only in such and no other re- <note>The text breaks here at the syllable "Er-," likely continuing on the next page as "Erscheinung" (manifestation/appearance) or "Erklärung" (explanation).</note>

# Fourth Conversation.

### Philolaus.

Here is your little book back, with my thanks. One hears Lessing speaking even when he only utters a few syllables; yet I cannot deny that I would have liked to hear him at greater length on our subject.

**Theophron.** I as well; however, how does the little he says please you?

**Philolaus.** It is too little to judge; and then again, too fragmented—even perhaps too forcefully stated here and there, in Lessing's characteristic manner <note>original: *Manier*; referring to Lessing's famously sharp, provocative, and often paradoxical style of writing and speaking.</note>. If you do not object: I will single out his words and state my opinion on them without any pretension.

**Theophron.** Do so. In doing this, you will merely become the commentator <term>commentator<gloss>An interpreter or expositor; someone who explains the meaning of a text.</gloss></term> of an author who can no longer explain himself to us <note>Lessing had died in 1781, several years before the publication of the book they are discussing, leaving his friends and critics to debate his true religious views.</note>.

<summary>Philolaus returns the book containing Lessing's thoughts on Spinoza and expresses a desire for more detail. He proposes to analyze Lessing's specific statements one by one, acting as a commentator for the deceased philosopher.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page, finishing the sentence describing how man achieves freedom through a specific kind of necessity.</meta>

of inner necessity—that is, through complete concepts <note>original: *vollständige Begriffe*; in Spinoza's philosophy, these are "adequate ideas" that allow the mind to understand the true cause of things</note> which the knowledge and love of God alone can grant us—to become masters over our passions, and indeed over *fate* itself. ...have seen, extension <note>original: *Ausdehnung*; in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to the primary attribute of matter—occupying space—as opposed to thought</note> does not belong at all), be grounded; who is there to tell us this? Lessing himself only says that such a force *could* exist; yet he admits himself that we are not capable of conceiving anything about it.

**Theophron.** It seems to me that Lessing has said too much there. What if I were to name for you not a higher force, but the real concept <note>original: *reellen Begriff*; a fundamental idea that corresponds to an actual reality, often used in Enlightenment philosophy to denote something that is not merely a mental construct</note> in which all these forces are not only grounded, but which they also collectively do not exhaust? It possesses all the qualities that Lessing demands of his unknown force; "it is infinitely more excellent than any individual effect of an individual force and truly provides a kind of enjoyment <note>original: *Genuss*; here suggesting an intellectual or spiritual satisfaction or the "fruition" of a truth</note> that not only transcends all concepts but also (not outside, but) above and before every concept, because every concept presupposes it and rests upon it."

**Philolaus.** And this concept is—?

<summary>Theophron challenges Lessing’s view that the "higher power" is inconceivable, suggesting instead that there is a "real concept" that grounds all forces and precedes all human thought.</summary>
<keywords>Theophron, Philolaus, Lessing, extension, higher force, real concept, philosophy of mind, grounding, intellectual enjoyment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Theophron and Philolaus. Theophron is responding to the previous question regarding the nature of the "real concept" that grounds all forces, identifying it as "Existence" (Daseyn). He contrasts Lessing’s incomplete understanding of Spinoza with a more holistic view of divine and human existence.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<sig>K 2</sig>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal divider consisting of a central floral or leaf-like motif with symmetrical scrolling lines extending to the left and right, ending in small circular flourishes.</image-desc>

**Theophron.** Existence. You see, Lessing stopped only halfway with Spinoza; otherwise, he would have already developed this concept for himself, which our philosopher <note>original: *Weltweise*; literally "world-wise one," a common 18th-century term for a philosopher</note> sufficiently presents as the ground and essence <note>original: *Inbegriff*; the sum total or quintessential core</note> of all forces. Existence is more excellent than any of its effects: it provides an enjoyment <note>original: *Genuß*; here referring to the internal satisfaction or "fruition" of being alive and conscious</note> that not only transcends individual concepts but cannot be measured by them at all: for the power of representation <note>original: *Vorstellungskraft*; the mental faculty of forming ideas or "images" of things</note> is only one of its powers, to which many other powers are subordinate. This is how it is with humans; it must be the same case with all limited beings; and with God?

**Philolaus.** In God’s existence, that which Lessing senses regarding this higher force—which is supposed to transcend all thought—applies in the most eminent way. His existence is the primal ground <note>original: *Urgrund*</note> of all reality, the essence of all forces, an enjoyment that transcends all concepts—

<summary>Theophron identifies "Existence" as the fundamental force that Lessing couldn't name, arguing it is the ground of all other faculties like imagination. Philolaus agrees, applying this to God as the ultimate source of reality and power.</summary>
<keywords>Theophron, Philolaus, Lessing, Spinoza, existence, primal ground, philosophy of religion, representation, divine nature</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Theophron and Philolaus concerning the philosophy of Baruch Spinoza and Gotthold Ephraim Lessing’s interpretation of it. The discussion centers on whether the "highest force" (God) possesses self-knowledge and how "existence" relates to "thought."</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Phi-</meta>

---

**Theophron.** One which also lies beyond all comprehension <note>original: *Begriff*</note>? You see once again that Lessing had not entirely untangled the knot of Spinozist ideas for himself. The highest force must know itself; otherwise, it would be a blind power that would certainly be overcome by a thinking one, and thus would not be the Godhead <note>original: *Gottheit*</note>.

**Philolaus.** “But he, Spinoza, was far from representing our wretched way of acting according to intentions as the highest method, and from placing thought at the very top.” a)

**Theophron.** Next to existence <note>original: *Daseyn*</note> as the ground of all forces, thought also stands at the top in his <note>meaning Spinoza's</note> view; he is merely far from attributing to the Infinite restricted **modes of representation**, **knowledge from experience** <note>original: *Känntnisse a posteriori*; knowledge derived from sensory observation rather than innate reason</note>, **fallible deliberations**, or **arbitrary intentions**; and it is precisely this that constitutes the excellence of his system.

---

a) P. 20.

<summary>Theophron argues that the divine "highest force" must possess self-knowledge to avoid being a "blind power," clarifying that Spinoza places thought at the highest level while stripping it of human limitations like fallibility and arbitrary intent.</summary>
<keywords>Spinoza, Lessing, Godhead, existence, self-knowledge, a posteriori, modes of representation, rationalism</keywords>

**Philolaus.** Lessing further asks: *a)* “according to what conceptions his friend <note>referring to Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, Lessing's contemporary</note> assumes a personal, **extramundane deity** <term>extramundane deity: a God who exists outside or beyond the physical universe, rather than being identical with it</term>? escape, against which he chose the more cautious expression of a **moral necessity** <term>moral necessity: Leibniz’s concept that God, while logically free to do otherwise, is "morally" bound to choose the best possible option because of His perfect goodness and wisdom</term>.

**Philolaus.** I wonder how such a sharp-witted man could be satisfied with that explanation.

are, the more our affects <note>original: *Affekten*; in the context of Spinoza and 18th-century philosophy, "affects" refers to emotions or passions that can cloud rational judgment</note> fall silent, the more willingly all human minds unite in the clearly-recognized truth: for there is only One Reason, only One Truth. Regarding Leibniz, however, I cannot conceal that he often seems to me too flexible, too rich in hypotheses. **Theophron.** Do not say that, my friend. <meta>This page continues the speech of Theophron from page 150. The text completes the word "Vernünfteln" (reasoning/sophistry) from the previous page, concluding the thought that abstract reasoning does not constitute the entirety of human nature.</meta>

reasoning, is not the whole substance of man. Substance and existence lie at the foundation of everything, even the noblest powers of our nature; these cannot be dissolved into empty reasoning <note>original: "Vernünftelei." This term implies a derogatory view of abstract, purely logical deduction that is detached from lived reality.</note> or even be reasoned away by it. Without existence and a series of existences, man would not think as he thinks; consequently, the purpose of his thoughts must be, not to dream up figments of the mind and to play with sham concepts and sham words as if they were a self-made reality; but rather, as he calls it, to reveal existence <note>original: "Daseyn." A foundational term in German philosophy referring to "being" or "presence."</note>, to accept it as something given or (in his expression) as a revelation of God, beyond which and behind which one cannot go. One must therefore purify and sharpen one's senses through experience, and one's inner sense through a love of truth, order, and coherence in thought, and renounce all arbitrary connections of non-existent sham concepts—that is, the sluggish, dead Nothingness.

<summary>Theophron argues that existence and "Daseyn" are the fundamental basis of human nature, which cannot be reduced to mere abstract reasoning. He posits that the goal of thought is to reveal the "revelation of God" inherent in existence rather than playing with empty, illusory concepts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 157. much less a demonstration. For even without taking into consideration the origin of the forces that think, act, and work—forces which the **transcendent** <note>original: "transscendente." <meta>This page continues Theophron’s argument from page 158. He is explaining that the very structure of logical thought—the way we connect a subject to a predicate—serves as an internal proof of a divine, orderly foundation for the universe.</meta>

predicate stands an **Is** or **Is not**, and this **Is**—this formula of equality and correspondence between different concepts, the mere sign **=**—is my demonstration of God. <note>In the 18th century, a "demonstration" was a formal logical or mathematical proof that left no room for doubt.</note> For to say it once more: there is a reason, a connection of the thinkable in the world according to unchanging rules; consequently, there must be an essential ground for this connection, even assuming that there were only a single thinking being. No one has arbitrarily devised the rule of this connection, just as no thinking being caught in space and time practices it according to their own whim; it is the necessary ground of their thoughts, as it is of all thoughts. In the spirit world, this rule is exactly what the law of equilibrium <note>The author uses a metaphor from physics: just as gravity and balance (equilibrium) govern physical bodies, logic governs the "world of spirits" or minds.</note> is among physical bodies: it carries its own inner necessity within itself. There exists, therefore, such an inner necessity—that is, an independent truth.

<note>Vocabulary: Predicate, demonstration of God, reason, connection of the thinkable, spirit world, equilibrium, necessity, independent truth.</note>

<summary>Theophron argues that the fundamental laws of logic and the "agreement" between concepts (the "Is" in a sentence) prove the existence of an underlying divine necessity. --- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron. In the preceding section, Theophron argued that the logical connection between concepts—the "is" that joins a subject and a predicate—serves as an internal proof of God's existence and the order of the universe. He now moves from the existence of this "independent truth" to where it resides and how humans interact with it despite our capacity for error.</meta>

**Philolaus.** But where does this independent truth dwell?

**Theophron.** In God and, in a derivative way, in everything to which He gave reality; it may dwell there either objectively or subjectively. <note>In the philosophical context of the late 18th century, "objective" truth refers to the reality of the thing as it exists in the world, while "subjective" truth refers to how that reality is represented within the human mind.</note> Our knowledge is drawn from the senses and from experience: we must therefore first merely perceive, hold various similarities together, and then distinguish and refine more general concepts from individual differences. This whole process is a path that makes errors in perception—as well as in the separating, connecting, and dividing of concepts—not only possible, but almost unavoidable: it is the necessary lot of humanity. Yet the rule according to which we perceive, distinguish, conclude, and connect is a divine rule: even in error we have acted according to it and were bound to act according to it, even if all the objects of our thought were but an illusion. <note>Theophron is making a distinction between "content" and "form." He argues that while the things we think about (content) might be wrong or hallucinatory, the logical laws we use to process them (form) are a "divine rule" that remains consistent and perfect.</note>

<summary>Theophron explains that while human experience is often flawed and prone to error, the underlying laws of logic and reason are divine. He asserts that truth resides in God and that the human mind follows divine rules of thought even when processing incorrect information.</summary>

<keywords>independent truth, divine rule, logic, epistemology, human error, perception, abstraction, God, reality, illusion</keywords>

Consider the truths of geometry. For our senses, there is perhaps no perfect circle in nature; but even if there were none, the idealized mathematical circle—with everything established, abstracted, and proven within it according to internal necessity—is for me the most perfect demonstration of an independent divine truth. <note>Theophron is using a Platonic argument here: even if a physical circle in the dirt is lumpy and imperfect, the *concept* of a circle is perfect and eternal, suggesting it comes from a divine source rather than just our messy physical experience.</note> It proves to me, namely, that there exists a mathematical Reason in the world; and since our senses only hinder us from recognizing and applying it everywhere in nature, this very Reason, according to its essence, tells us that if there are thinking beings who view the world with finer senses, they think according to these same unique and necessary rules.
