<meta>This page continues Theophron’s argument from the bottom of page 162. Having asserted that the concept of Reason itself contains the proof of God, he now explains how this internal logical necessity serves as the hidden foundation for all other types of philosophical proofs.</meta>

I venture, my friend <note>Theophron is addressing his dialogue partner, Philolaus.</note>, to say that this is the only essential demonstration <note>In this context, a "demonstration" is a formal logical proof that moves from known premises to an inescapable conclusion.</note> of God (and indeed there cannot be several essential ones) which recurs in all proofs; yet it appears nowhere so sharply and purely as in the laws of our understanding.

All proofs—for example, those drawn from nature, where we perceive necessary laws of motion and rest, the persistence of things according to a proportion of their internal forces, and so forth—rest upon the same rule that we notice most purely in our reason, namely: “that every thing is what it is, that its essence rests on forces, its persistence on a balance of these forces, its effect on their relationships to other things; and indeed all of this arises not from arbitrary intentions, which we set entirely aside, but from internal laws of necessity, from which persistence and destruction, composition and dissolution, motion, rest

...no demonstration of any existence—not just of a single one—is possible. But what have you done by this, other than abolish existence, the very ground of all thinking? And how is a sound philosophy further possible? If my senses already convince me of existence in their way—that is, in a dark and confused manner <note>The distinction between "dark and confused" (*dunkel und verworren*) sensory perception versus "clear and distinct" rational concepts is a hallmark of Enlightenment rationalism, particularly the school of Leibniz and Christian Wolff.</note>—how should my reason not be able to convince me of existence in its way, namely through clear and complete concepts? 

But if I demand of reason that it give me its concepts as **sensory intuitions** <term>sensory intuitions (*sinnliche Anschauungen*)</term> without sensory intuition, or that it demonstrate the existence of sensory objects (which do not belong to its domain) as pure truths of reason, and I reproach it for not being willing or able to do so: then my reproach has no more basis than if I wished to hear color, taste light, or see sound. Let us take care, Philolaus, that we never fall into this region of **hypercriticism** <note>Hypercriticism (*Hyperkritik*) refers to an over-extended skepticism that critiques the very foundations of human understanding, a term often used by critics of Immanuel Kant to suggest his philosophy went too far in questioning reality.</note> of sound understanding— <meta>The catchword "gerat" indicates the sentence will conclude on the next page with the word "geraten," meaning to fall into or arrive at a state.</meta>

<summary>Theophron argues that reason and the senses provide different but equally valid paths to understanding existence. He warns Philolaus that applying the wrong standards to reason leads to a "hypercriticism" that destroys the basis of all sound philosophy.</summary>

<keywords>existence, reason, senses, clear and distinct concepts, sensory intuition, hypercriticism, sound understanding, Philolaus, Theophron</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron from page 164. Theophron is explaining why causality—the relationship between cause and effect—is a matter of sensory experience and conjecture rather than mathematical or logical certainty.</meta>

found one another. You are well aware of the **erroneous conjectures** <note>original: "Fehl-Muthmaaßungen"; mistakes in reasoning based on assumptions rather than proof.</note> that have often been made about this, even in the course of daily experience regarding the most common things. The reason for this is clear: every inference from **cause to effect** <term>cause and effect (*Ursache und Wirkung*): the relationship where one event (the cause) makes another happen (the effect). Herder argues we see the sequence of events, but not the actual "link" or power connecting them.</term>, or conversely from effect to cause, was never a **demonstration** <term>demonstration (*Demonstration*): a rigorous logical proof that provides absolute certainty, such as in geometry.</term>, but always only a conjecture within the realm of the **senses** <note>original: "Sinnlichkeiten"; the world as perceived through our physical senses.</note>.

We do not know what **power** <term>power (*Kraft*): a central concept in Herder’s philosophy, referring to the active, invisible energy or "force" that drives life and thought. We see its effect only as spectators and therefore form for ourselves **analogical judgments** <note>judgments based on similarities or comparisons between different things rather than direct knowledge of their inner essence.</note>. Even the general rules on this subject, which we find most well-proven, we can never strictly demonstrate. 

What should we know more intimately than the power that thinks and works within us? Yet we know it just as little as any other power that exists outside of us. Even the thoughts of my **soul**, when considered as effects, I do not comprehend; they are only comprehensible to me when I consider them "as eternal truths belonging to the essence of my **reason**" among

**Theophron.** Consider the enormous consequence of such a deceptive image: God, as the soul of the whole, would be an effect—nothing but an effect of the world. Consequently, they would likely be only the effects of a composition without anything doing the composing; thus the body—that is, the world—becomes the creator, and God becomes a creature <note>Theophron is pointing out a logical reversal: if God is merely the "soul" produced by the universe's organization, then the material universe is the primary cause, and God is merely its byproduct.</note>. **Philolaus.** „The organic scope of the same (the soul) could not be conceived according to the analogy of the organic parts of this scope, insofar as it could relate to nothing that existed outside of it, take from it, and give back to it.„ <note>Philolaus is quoting Jacobi's *On the Doctrine of Spinoza*. > Infinity, who measures you?
> For you, worlds are days and humans moments.
> Perhaps the thousandth of the suns rolls on now
> and a thousand still remain behind.
> Like a clock, animated by a weight,
> a sun hurries, moved by God's power;
> its drive runs out and another strikes,
> But you remain and do not count them.

**Philolaus.** So I would like, then, without any images, to become acquainted with the laws of nature of God’s economy <term>Haushaltung: In this context, it refers to "divine economy," the way God organizes and governs the universe according to rational laws.</term>, symbols that express the highest reality, a necessary goodness and wisdom. For you, Theophron, as for me, an illumination or repetition of such things—of the eternal ideas in the divine intellect—must be a source of joy.

**Theophron.** We shall choose tomorrow’s evening hour for that. Are you already familiar with this hymn? It gives you no image of God, it is true; but perhaps something better than images.

### G o d. *)

The Only One, who is everything to all,
is our God! Creatures, worship Him.
The Uncreated, the Only One,
the First—Him, O creatures, worship.

You, His great, vast, beautiful world,

with His throne. He neither sits nor stands;

the Essence of all essences; He is God, is our God! Creatures, worship Him.

Who is there, whom He called into His workshop, to step forward and see, to see—how He does it? How He holds the ocean in such supple obedience that not a single drop of its water wishes to leave its depths; how He binds the moon to a thin thread and lets it float in the blue air; how in the time of a horse’s or rider’s dash <note>The original German "Huy" is an exclamation for sudden, swift movement, similar to "zip" or "in a flash."</note> He measures ten thousand million solar distances and misses not an apple, nor a speck of dust! <note>This highlights the Enlightenment-era fascination with a "Clockmaker" God who manages both the vastness of space and the smallest earthly details with perfect precision.</note>

<meta>This page begins the "Fifth Conversation" of the work. It follows the conclusion of a long hymn on the previous page that celebrated the "Great Chain of Being" and the omnipresence of God. # Fifth Conversation

->T h e a n o<-

Allow me, my friends, to be your visible listener today, as I have been invisible until now. Much of your conversations I have not understood, and today too I do not desire to understand everything; it is enough for me if I simply follow the general sense of your discourse <note>original: "Unterredung." This refers to a structured, philosophical dialogue common in Enlightenment literature.</note>. My presence should not disturb you; I will perform my work <note>In the 18th-century context, "work" (Arbeit) for a woman in this setting usually referred to domestic handiwork like knitting or embroidery, which allowed her to listen to intellectual debate while remaining modest.</note> in silence and accompany you only with my thoughts.

**Theophron.** You are welcome to our conversation, Theano: for you surely have nothing against Theano listening to us, Philolaus?

**Philolaus.** Quite a lot, if she intended to do so merely in silence. You must join in our conversation and, when it <note>The sentence breaks off here as the text continues on the next page.</note>

with such gentle love and joy that we not only think of our own destruction with reluctance, but are also unable to imagine it for ourselves with all our might. For example, instead of the full space of the world, he can imagine a vast, black, empty space; but with that, he still has not imagined **Nothingness**. **Philolaus.** Or if even existence—the joyful, necessary existence of God—seems hideous to them—"Pure necessity," they say, "as the ultimate supporter <note>original: "Träger" (bearer or supporter)</note> of all things, is an abyss for reason." Even Haller’s <note>Albrecht von Haller (1708–1777), a Swiss scientist and poet famous for his poem "Eternity," which describes the overwhelming nature of infinite time.</note> *Eternity* does not make nearly as dizzying an impression on the soul as the necessary existence of God: for while the former measures, it does not have to support anything. One cannot bear the thought that a being, whom we imagine as the highest among all possible beings, says as it were to himself: "I am from eternity to eternity: outside of me is nothing, except that which is something merely through my will; but from where, then, am I?" <summary>Philolaus discusses the philosophical "abyss of reason," where the concept of God's necessary and eternal existence becomes more overwhelming to the human mind than even the infinite span of eternity itself.</summary>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philosophical argument Philolaus was recounting regarding the "abyss of reason," where even the concept of God seems to vanish into nothingness. Theano then interjects, rejecting these cold abstractions in favor of a more meaningful view of existence.</meta>

**Theano.** Rescue me, Theophron, from the desolate ideas that Philolaus is citing. Theophron. And what a pledge, my friends, we possess with this gift for the entire duration of our lives. Existence <note>original: "Daseyn" (being or presence)</note> is an indivisible concept, an essence. It can no more be transformed into nothingness than it is itself a nothing, otherwise the Deity <note>original: "Gottheit"</note> could destroy itself. I am not speaking here of appearances, of the compositions of any form within that which we call space and time. Everything that appears must also disappear; every growth of time also carries within itself the seed of decay, which ensures that it does not last forever in its outward appearance. That which is composed is eventually dissolved: for this very composition and dissolution is called world order <term>Weltordnung: the organized structure or natural laws governing the universe</term> and is the ever-active life of the World-Spirit <term>Weltgeist: a central concept in German Idealism referring to an animating cosmic force or collective consciousness that drives history and nature</term>. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philolaus and Theophron regarding the nature of matter and the soul. Philolaus is expanding on his realization that matter is not a mere sensory illusion but a reality formed by the "intimate connection of acting forces."</meta>

connected only to the ideas of representing creatures; it exists so through its own nature and truth, through the intimate connection of acting forces. Nothing in nature stands alone: nothing is without cause, nothing without effect; and since everything exists in connection and all that is possible is present, nothing in nature is without organization. Every force stands in connection with other forces that either serve it or rule over it. If my soul, then, is a substantial force and its current realm of action is destroyed, it can never lack a new organ in a creation where no gap, no leap, and no isolated island exists. <note>Herder is invoking the "Law of Continuity" (*lex continui*), the philosophical belief that nature is a continuous scale without "leaps" (*saltus*) or voids. If the soul is a natural force, it must logically transition into a new state rather than simply vanishing.</note> New serving forces will assist it and—within its new connection to a world where everything is interconnected—will form its new <term>sphere of action: *Wirkungskreis*; the specific environment or domain where a force or soul exerts its influence and expresses its nature.</term>

**Theophron.** And what necessary duty flows from this, dear Philolaus, to ensure that the soul, in its interior, in the system of its

<summary>Philolaus argues that because nature is a perfect continuum of cause and effect, a soul—as a "substantial force"—will always find new "organs" or systems of force to inhabit even after its current body is destroyed.</summary>

First, then. Every being is what it is and has neither a concept of nothingness, nor a longing for it. <note>The author suggests that existence is inherently positive; life naturally seeks to continue itself and cannot truly conceive of its own non-existence.</note> All perfection of a thing is its reality; the feeling of this reality is the indwelling reward of its existence, its inner joy. <meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the nature of magnetism as a metaphor for universal laws. Theano and Theophron are joined by Philolaus as they expand the concept of magnetic poles to a general law of physics and geometry.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

**Theano.** I know it, and that if one changes them, one changes both of them <note>Theano refers to the scientific principle that magnetic poles are inseparable; if you flip or alter the polarity of one end, the other must change accordingly.</note>.

**Theophron.** In the magnet, therefore, you have the most beautiful image of what hate and love are in creation; and I am certain that the same will be discovered in several, and perhaps in all, fluids <note>In the 18th century, "fluids" often referred to "imponderable" substances like electricity, heat, and magnetism, which were thought to behave like liquids flowing through space or matter.</note>.

<summary>Theophron explains how magnetism provides a geometric model for all forces in nature, illustrating how energy organizes itself into opposing poles around an axis. ...of this construction lay according to fixed rules in the **operative forces** <term>operative forces|German: "Wirkungskräfte." These are the active powers within a physical system that produce effects or motion.</term> of the system that was thus formed. Just as a sphere cannot have a North Pole without a South Pole: so too, in a system of forces that forms regularly, there cannot be a shape in which the **friendly** and the **hostile** <note>The speaker uses "friendly" and "hostile" (original: "Freundschaftliche und Feindschaftliche") as poetic metaphors for the physical principles of attraction and repulsion.</note> are not separated; they thereby form a whole through the counter-balance that both provide to one another according to increasing and decreasing degrees of connection.

It is likely that no system of electrical forces could exist in the world if there were not two opposing electricities, which have indeed been found through experience. The same is true of heat and cold: the same is likely true of every system of forces, which can only maintain unity through diversity and connection through opposition. Observational **natural philosophy** <term>natural philosophy|German: "Naturlehre." In the 18th century, this term encompassed the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern physics.</term>, which is still so young,

<meta>The previous page concluded with a mention of "natural philosophy, which is still so young,". This page continues that thought, suggesting that as this field of study matures, it will eventually replace notions of divine caprice or random chance with a comprehensive understanding of universal natural laws.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>

**is,** and will one day reach so far in all these matters that it will finally banish every blind **arbitrariness** <term>arbitrariness|German: "Willkühr." This refers to the idea of things happening by chance or by a capricious, unpredictable will, rather than through fixed, rational laws.</term> from the world, under which everything would fall apart and, in essence, all laws of nature would cease to exist. For you must grant me, my friends: if the magnet, the electrical force, light, heat and cold, attraction, **gravity** <term>gravity|German: "Schwere." In the late 18th century, this term referred to the general quality of weight or the universal force of attraction described by Newton.</term>, and so forth, acted arbitrarily; if the triangle were no triangle, and the circle no circle: then we might as well declare all observations of physics and mathematics to be nonsense and wait for **arbitrary revelations** <note>The author is contrasting the "revelations" claimed by religious mysticism or those who believe in constant divine intervention (miracles) with the predictable, discoverable "laws" of the physical sciences.</note>. 

But if it is certain that we have already found mathematically precise laws of nature for so many forces: who would wish to set a boundary for us where they are no longer to be found, but where the blind will of God begins? In creation everything is connection, everything is order; if, therefore, even a single law of nature exists anywhere within it, then laws of nature must rule everywhere, or else creation falls apart like a chaos.

<summary>The author argues for the absolute universality of natural laws, asserting that if any part of the universe follows mathematical rules, the entire cosmos must be a structured system rather than the result of divine whim.</summary>

**Theano.** But you are departing, my friend, from the law of hate and love <note>Theano refers to the ancient Greek concepts of *Philia* (Love/Attraction) and *Neikos* (Strife/Repulsion) proposed by the philosopher Empedocles as the two primary forces driving the universe.</note>, where according to your system one cannot exist without the other.

**Theophron.** Because everything that can exist in the world is present, the opposite must also be there; and a law of supreme wisdom must form a system from these opposites—from the North and South Poles everywhere. In every circle of nature, the **chart of the thirty-two winds** <term>chart of the thirty-two winds|German: "Tafel der zwei und dreissig Winde." This refers to the traditional compass rose, representing all possible directions of the wind and, metaphorically, all possible states of a system.</term> is present; in every line of a sunbeam, the entire **pyramid of colors** <note>This refers to the spectrum of light. Everything attracts or repels or remains indifferent toward one another, and the axis of these active forces runs connected through all

**Theano.** I see a beautiful glimmer, Theophron; but I do not yet see the dawn.

Not even the tiniest particle of a leaf can be idle for a single moment, or there would be death in creation. The inherent drive of a living being to maintain its own identity and state of being despite external changes.</term>. And since in the realm of change nothing can remain unchanged, and yet everything wants to and must maintain its existence <term>existence|German: *Daseyn*. <summary>Theophron explains that life is defined by the restless activity of internal forces that constantly "re-dress" themselves in new organic forms to maintain their existence. ...Theophron's system of a truth and goodness necessary in itself seem to follow for me irrefutably. I stopped at the second proposition; so:

III. All forces of nature act organically. Every organization <term>organization|German: *Organisation*. In the context of 18th-century vitalism, this refers to a living biological system or structure through which vital forces are channeled and directed.</term> is nothing but a system of living forces which, according to eternal rules of wisdom, goodness, and beauty, serve a primary force.

IV. The laws according to which this [primary force] rules and those [subordinate forces] serve are: the inner persistence of every being, union with like things and separation from the opposite, and finally, assimilation <note>original: *Verähnlichung*. This describes the process by which a living being takes in external elements and transforms them to match its own nature, such as in nutrition or growth.</note> to itself and the imprint of its essence in another. They are effects through which the Deity itself has been revealed, and no others, no higher ones, are conceivable.

V. No death exists in creation, but transformation; transformation according to the wisest, best law of necessity <term>necessity|German: *Nothwendigkeit*. In this philosophical context, necessity refers to the logical and natural laws that must be followed for the universe to function rationally.</term>, according to which every force <term>force|German: *Kraft*. A central term in 18th-century vitalism, referring to the inherent energy or soul-like power within all matter that drives life and change.</term> in the realm of changes wishes to preserve itself as always new, always active, and thus through attraction and repulsion, through friendship and enmity, unceasingly changes its organic garment <note>original: *organisches Gewand*. This metaphor suggests that the physical body is merely an outer clothing for the eternal, active force within.</note>. <note>original: *Anziehen und Abstoßen, durch Freundschaft und Feindschaft*. These terms link physical laws (attraction and repulsion) with emotional or moral ones (friendship and enmity), suggesting a unified law for both the physical and spiritual worlds.</note>

VI. No rest exists in creation: for an idle rest would be death. Every living force acts and continues to act: with every continued effect, it therefore strides further and works itself out, according to inner eternal rules of wisdom and goodness that press upon it, which lie within it.

VII. The more it works itself out, the more it also acts upon others, expands its boundaries, organizes and imprints upon them the image of goodness and beauty,

In the whole of Nature, therefore, one **necessary law** <term>necessary law|German: *nothwendiges Gesetz*. A central concept in Enlightenment philosophy, suggesting that the laws of the universe are not arbitrary but follow a logical, inescapable order.</term> reigns: that from chaos, **order** arises; from dormant capacities, **active forces** emerge. ### VIII.
In the **Kingdom of God**, therefore, nothing **evil** exists that possesses any true reality. All evil is a nothingness <note>The author is following a tradition (common in thinkers like Leibniz) which argues that "evil" is not a "thing" created by God, but rather a lack or "privation" of good.</note>; however, we apply the name of **evil** to that which is merely a **limit**, or an **opposition**, or a **transition**—and none of these three deserves that name.

I might wish, Theophron, to speak with you further on this point; a **theodicy** <term>theodicy|A philosophical attempt to reconcile the existence of evil with an all-powerful, good Creator.</term> of wise **necessity** is in my thoughts.

### IX.
Just as limits belong to the measure of every **existence** in **space** and in **time**, so too in the **Kingdom of God**—where everything is **Spirit**—the opposite must also exist:

...must: so it belongs to the highest **goodness** of this kingdom that even opposites help and promote one another: for only through the union of both does a world in every **substance** <term>substance|German: *Substanz*. In 18th-century philosophy, particularly Spinozism, this refers to an independent, fundamental reality that exists in itself.</term>—that is, a subsisting, whole **existence**—become complete in goodness as well as in **beauty**.

### X.
Even the **faults** of men are good for an understanding spirit: for they must soon reveal themselves to him as faults and thus, like contrasts <note>The author uses a visual metaphor here: just as shadows make light appear brighter in a painting, human errors make truth and goodness more distinct.</note>, help him toward more light, toward purer goodness and truth. And all this, too, happens not as mere **whim** <term>whim|German: *Willkühr*. The idea that the universe is governed by random choice or an arbitrary deity, which the author rejects in favor of logical laws.</term>, but according to eternal laws of **reason**, **order**, and **goodness**.

Are you satisfied with my conclusions, Theophron?

**Theophron.** Completely. Your acute mind always runs ahead of me, Philolaus;

<summary>Philolaus concludes his philosophical argument by asserting that even human errors and opposing forces serve a higher purpose, acting as necessary contrasts that reveal truth and beauty under the eternal laws of reason.</summary>

This refers to the mechanical memorization of philosophical dogmas or systems without true internal understanding.</note>, and true philosophy must never be learned by heart.

**Theano.** I wish that rule for my brother. For some time now, he has been preoccupied with a philosophy that confuses his head—and mine too—whenever he speaks of it.
